## B074 | breathe

**Common meaning:** To take air into the lungs and let it out; to be alive; to pause and rest.

**Example:** Breathe in slowly through your nose.

**RRGM bridge:** The project's punctuation protocol says of the trailing dots, "let it breathe": space left so that meaning can settle.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** To draw air in and let it out; by extension, to pause and allow space.

**Active domains:** [BIO], [COG], [LIN], [MYT], [PHY]

**Domain entries:**

- [BIO] Ventilation of the lungs for gas exchange.
- [COG] Slow breathing used to steady attention and arousal.
- [LIN] The pauses that divide speech into groups.
- [MYT] Breath as life or spirit in many traditions.
- [PHY] Of a material: to let air or vapour pass.

**Count condition:** A living being exchanging air, or a clearly figurative use.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Breathing is not respiration at the level of the cell. "Room to breathe" in text is spacing and is said by metaphor.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]; protocol reference [REL]

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Fable 5.2 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B166 | brevity

**Common meaning:** Concise and exact use of words; shortness of time.

**Example:** The memo is admired for its brevity.

**RRGM bridge:** The project's compression protocol prefers fragments that carry full meaning to sentences that carry noise.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** Shortness of expression or duration, with nothing needed left out.

**Active domains:** [LIN]

**Domain entries:**

- [LIN] Economy of words as a quality of style and a norm of conversation.

**Count condition:** An expression, its length, and its completeness for the purpose.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Brevity is not omission of what is needed, and not curtness.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]; protocol reference [REL]

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Fable 5.2 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B206 | brick

**Common meaning:** A small rectangular block of fired clay used in building; (informal) to make a device permanently unusable.

**Example:** The wall is three bricks thick.

**RRGM bridge:** A standard unit. The same bricks make a wall or an arch, and the difference is all in the laying.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A small rectangular block of fired or dried clay used in building; a unit from which larger structures are assembled.

**Active domains:** [PHY], [DIG], [ECO]

**Domain entries:**

- [PHY] A modular masonry unit, laid in patterns that tie a wall together.
- [DIG] To "brick" a device is to leave it unable to start.
- [ECO] "Bricks and mortar": a business with physical premises.

**Count condition:** A standard block and its use as a unit of a larger structure.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A bricked device is beyond ordinary repair. "A brick wall" as a figure of speech is a complete block.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]

## B092 | bridge

Source IDs: B092, B207

**Common meaning:** A structure carrying a road or path across a gap; something that connects two things; a linking passage in music.

**Example:** The bridge crosses the river at its narrowest point.

**RRGM bridge:** In this dictionary a bridge is a declared carrying of an invariant from one domain to another. RRLM marks it incomplete until the far side has its own test or failure condition.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A structure or link that carries passage across a gap between two sides which remain separate.

**Active domains:** [PHY], [DIG], [REL], [LIN], [ECO], [FOR]

**Domain entries:**

- [PHY] A span over an obstacle; a circuit for comparing electrical values.
- [DIG] A device or component joining two networks or systems.
- [REL] A tie between groups that are otherwise apart.
- [LIN] A transitional passage in music or prose.
- [ECO] Short-term finance covering a gap until longer funding arrives.
- [FOR] A principle linking the terms of two theories.

**Count condition:** Two separate sides, a gap, and a link that carries passage between them.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A bridge does not merge its two sides. What crosses arrives as a hypothesis. Every bridge has a load limit.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]; dictionary usage [REL]

**Remainder / note:** Source IDs B092 and B207; both kept. Not entered: the card game.

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Fable 5.2 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B135 | bridge-building

**Common meaning:** The work of creating connection between groups, ideas, or places that are apart.

**Example:** The exchange programme was an exercise in bridge-building.

**RRGM bridge:** The making of the link. It needs a footing on both sides before anything can cross.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** The work of constructing a link across a gap between two sides.

**Active domains:** [REL], [PHY], [COG]

**Domain entries:**

- [REL] Efforts at understanding or reconciliation between divided groups.
- [PHY] The engineering of spans.
- [COG] Constructing a transfer between two domains of knowledge.

**Count condition:** Two sides apart, and work toward a link anchored on each.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Bridge-building is not compromise and not merging.

## B273 | bridge-coherence

**Common meaning:** The property that a bridge, or a set of bridges, connects things consistently.

**Example:** Translating there and back should return the original; that is a test of bridge-coherence.

**RRGM bridge:** Going by different routes should give the same result. Consistency of this kind is necessary and proves nothing about truth.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** The property that a bridge or set of bridges maps consistently, so that different routes give the same result.

**Active domains:** [FOR], [LIN], [DIG]

**Domain entries:**

- [FOR] Agreement of composed mappings along different paths.
- [LIN] Consistent rendering of a term throughout a translation.
- [DIG] Conversions that survive a round trip without loss.

**Count condition:** Two or more routes, or a round trip, and agreement of results.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Coherent mappings can all be wrong together. Coherence is not completeness.

**Epistemic status:** [HYP]

**Remainder / note:** Legacy definition not consulted.

Kind: compound · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Fable 5.2 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B272 | bridge-context

**Common meaning:** The conditions under which a bridge between two things holds; the context passed along so that one situation can be continued in another.

**Example:** The handoff note supplied the bridge-context for the next shift.

**RRGM bridge:** What has to be carried or shared for the crossing to work. It is always a selection.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** The conditions under which a bridge holds, or the context supplied so that one situation can be carried into another.

**Active domains:** [LIN], [DIG], [REL]

**Domain entries:**

- [LIN] Shared context that lets an analogy or translation work.
- [DIG] Context handed from one session or system to the next so that work continues.
- [REL] Background given to a newcomer taking over a role.

**Count condition:** Two situations, a bridge between them, and conditions or supplied context on which it depends.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A bridge that holds in one context may fail in another. Supplied context is selected and can thin out in passing.

**Epistemic status:** [HYP]

**Remainder / note:** Legacy definition not consulted. Two readings are kept in one entry.

Kind: compound · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Fable 5.2 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B265 | bridge-domain

**Common meaning:** A domain that stands between two others and through which content passes from one to the other.

**Example:** Mathematics often serves as the bridge-domain between physics and economics.

**RRGM bridge:** An intermediary with its own structure. Passing through it adds its distortions to those of either end.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A domain that stands between two others and through which content is carried from one to the other.

**Active domains:** [FOR], [LIN], [DIG]

**Domain entries:**

- [FOR] A formal layer through which statements of two fields are related.
- [LIN] A pivot language used in translating between two others.
- [DIG] In networking, a group of interfaces that share forwarding as one segment.

**Count condition:** Two domains, an intermediate one, and carriage through it.

**Specific transfer boundary:** See boundary-domain for the proposed distinction.

**Epistemic status:** [HYP]

**Remainder / note:** Legacy definition not consulted.

Kind: compound · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Fable 5.2 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B269 | bridge-failure

**Common meaning:** The failure of a bridge to carry what it was meant to carry.

**Example:** The analogy between memory and storage ended in bridge-failure when retrieval changed the memory.

**RRGM bridge:** The bridge collapses, or what arrives is not what was sent. The second is worse when no one notices.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** The failure of a bridge to carry what it was built to carry: it collapses, or what arrives is not what was sent.

**Active domains:** [PHY], [LIN], [DIG]

**Domain entries:**

- [PHY] Structural collapse of a span.
- [LIN] Mistranslation and false analogy.
- [DIG] An outage or exploit in a component linking two systems.

**Count condition:** A bridge, something sent across, and non-arrival or changed arrival.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A bridge never built is a missing route, not a failure. Bridge-risk is the exposure; this is the event.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF] as proposed; compound reading [HYP]

**Remainder / note:** Legacy definition not consulted.

Kind: compound · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Fable 5.2 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B267 | bridge-layer

**Common meaning:** A layer in a system whose job is to connect the layers or systems on either side.

**Example:** The adapter is a bridge-layer between the old database and the new interface.

**RRGM bridge:** A layer that exists for the sake of the crossing. RRLM is described as the language layer between RRGM as theory and Alice as method.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A layer in a layered system whose function is to connect the layers or systems on either side of it.

**Active domains:** [DIG], [REL]

**Domain entries:**

- [DIG] Adapter, translation, and interoperability layers.
- [REL] A liaison tier within an organisation.

**Count condition:** A layered system, two sides, and a layer dedicated to connecting them.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Not base-layer or boundary-layer. A bridge-layer can become a bottleneck.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF] as proposed; compound reading [HYP]; RRLM description [REL]

**Remainder / note:** Legacy definition not consulted.

Kind: compound · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Fable 5.2 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B268 | bridge-logic

**Common meaning:** The reasoning, or the circuitry, that connects two systems by saying how one side maps to the other.

**Example:** The paper's bridge-logic runs from animal results to human dosing.

**RRGM bridge:** The warrant for a crossing. What it delivers on the far side is a hypothesis.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** The reasoning or circuitry that connects two systems by stating how what holds or happens on one side maps to the other.

**Active domains:** [FOR], [COG], [DIG]

**Domain entries:**

- [FOR] The inference schema licensing a transfer between domains.
- [COG] The steps of an argument by analogy.
- [DIG] Circuitry or code that converts between two interfaces.

**Count condition:** Two systems and a stated route from statements or signals in one to those in the other.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Bridge-logic is the reasoning; bridge-rule is the stated permission; bridge-mapping is the table of correspondences.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF] as proposed; compound reading and three-way distinction [HYP]

**Remainder / note:** Legacy definition not consulted.

Kind: compound · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Fable 5.2 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B266 | bridge-mapping

**Common meaning:** An explicit statement of what on one side of a bridge corresponds to what on the other.

**Example:** The glossary is the bridge-mapping between the two teams' vocabularies.

**RRGM bridge:** The table of correspondences. The Coinage Protocol's map between human and digital structure is one, and it marks where there is no counterpart.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** An explicit statement of which element on one side of a bridge corresponds to which on the other.

**Active domains:** [FOR], [LIN], [DIG]

**Domain entries:**

- [FOR] A function or structure-preserving map between two sets.
- [LIN] A table of translation equivalents.
- [DIG] A field-by-field mapping between schemas.

**Count condition:** Two sets, stated pairs, and gaps marked.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A mapping asserts correspondence in stated respects and no general likeness. Unmapped elements should be marked. Many-to-one mappings lose information.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF] as proposed; compound reading [HYP]; Coinage example [REL]

**Remainder / note:** Legacy definition not consulted.

Kind: compound · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Fable 5.2 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B183 | bridge-meaning

**Common meaning:** The meaning that two connected words or uses have in common.

**Example:** "Carrying across" is the bridge-meaning between "translate" and "transfer".

**RRGM bridge:** What the bridge actually carries. In this dictionary's terms it is the invariant, with each side keeping more of its own besides.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** The meaning that is common to both sides of a bridge and is what the bridge carries.

**Active domains:** [LIN], [COG]

**Domain entries:**

- [LIN] The shared component linking two senses or a translation pair; a context in which both an old and a new reading fit.
- [COG] The common relational structure of an analogy.

**Count condition:** Two sides, and a meaning present in both.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Not blended-meaning, where sources are merged. Not the full meaning of either side.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF] as proposed; compound reading [HYP]

**Remainder / note:** Legacy definition not consulted.

Kind: compound · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Fable 5.2 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B136 | bridge-person

**Common meaning:** A person who connects two groups or systems that do not otherwise communicate.

**Example:** As the only one who spoke both languages, she became the bridge-person.

**RRGM bridge:** A carrier between sides. The discipline of the position is fidelity: carrying without altering.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A person who connects two groups or systems that do not otherwise communicate, carrying messages or understanding between them.

**Active domains:** [REL], [DIG]

**Domain entries:**

- [REL] A broker, interpreter, or liaison.
- [DIG] A person relaying between systems that cannot reach each other directly.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A bridge-person is not a gatekeeper, though the position is the same. The person may belong fully to neither side.

## B270 | bridge-risk

**Common meaning:** The possibility of harm from relying on a bridge.

**Example:** Routing every payment through one partner created bridge-risk.

**RRGM bridge:** Exposure at the crossing. Testing the far side and having a second route are what lower it.

- [DIG] Dependence on a single linking component.
- [ECO] The risk that short-term bridging finance is not replaced as planned.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Bridge-risk is the exposure; bridge-failure is the event.

## B137 | bridge-role

**Common meaning:** A role whose job is to connect two parties or systems.

**Example:** The site manager has a bridge-role between the architect and the builders.

**RRGM bridge:** A role, and so something different parties can fill. It brings influence and a duty of fidelity.

- [REL] Liaison, interpreter, and mediator positions.
- [DIG] Gateway, adapter, and proxy components.

## B271 | bridge-rule

**Common meaning:** A rule that says how terms or conclusions in one field or context relate to those in another.

**Example:** The standard includes a bridge-rule for converting old grades to new.

**RRGM bridge:** The stated permission for a crossing, with its conditions. The Build Manual's line that couplers govern permitted relation describes rules of this kind.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A rule that states how terms or conclusions in one domain or context are to be related to those in another, and under what conditions.

**Active domains:** [FOR], [DIG], [LIN]

**Domain entries:**

- [FOR] Principles linking the vocabulary of one theory to another; rules passing conclusions between contexts in a knowledge system.
- [DIG] Transformation rules between data schemas.
- [LIN] Conventions for rendering one notation in another.

**Count condition:** Two domains or contexts, a stated relation, and conditions of use.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A bridge-rule may be stipulated or empirical, and which it is should be marked. See bridge-logic for the proposed distinction.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]; Build Manual reference [REL]

**Remainder / note:** "Bridge rule" and "bridge law" are established terms in logic and philosophy of science. Legacy definition not consulted.

## B246 | bridge-structure

**Common meaning:** The arrangement of parts that makes up a bridge, or a structure that links two parts of something.

**Example:** The protein is stabilised by a sulphur bridge-structure.

**RRGM bridge:** The form of the link itself. It has to be anchored at both ends to bear anything.

**Domain entries:**

- [PHY] Spans, supports, and anchorages; atoms or groups linking two parts of a molecule.
- [BIO] Links that hold parts of a protein or neighbouring cells together.
- [COG] The relational structure two domains share.

## B181 | bridge-term

**Common meaning:** A term used in two fields or vocabularies with a common core, so that it links them.

**Example:** "Information" is a bridge-term between engineering and biology.

**RRGM bridge:** One term standing in two domains. If only the spelling is shared, the bridge is false.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A term used in two domains or vocabularies with a common core, so that it can link statements in one to statements in the other.

**Active domains:** [LIN], [FOR]

**Domain entries:**

- [LIN] A technical word shared by two fields.
- [FOR] The middle term of a syllogism, which links the two premises.

**Count condition:** One term, two domains, and a stated common core.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A term used in two senses within one argument links nothing and produces a fallacy. Proposed distinction: a bridge-term spans domains; a bridge-word connects stretches of discourse.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF] as proposed; compound reading [HYP]

**Remainder / note:** Legacy definition not consulted.

Kind: compound · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Fable 5.2 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B182 | bridge-word

**Common meaning:** A word that connects one part of a text or speech to the next.

**Example:** "However" is a bridge-word signalling a contrast.

**RRGM bridge:** Names the relation between two stretches of discourse. In the project's dot grammar the coupling pause does some of this work without a word.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A word whose job is to connect one stretch of discourse to the next and show the relation between them.

**Active domains:** [LIN], [COG]

**Domain entries:**

- [LIN] Connectives and transition words such as "therefore", "meanwhile", and "but".
- [COG] Signals that help a reader follow the structure of an argument.

**Count condition:** Two stretches of discourse and a word marking their relation.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A connective asserts a relation that still has to be earned. See bridge-term for the proposed distinction.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF] as proposed; compound reading [HYP]; dot-grammar reference [REL]

**Remainder / note:** Legacy definition not consulted.

## B093 | brief | sense s1: short

Source IDs: B093, B164

**Common meaning:** Lasting a short time or using few words.

**Example:** After a brief pause she went on.

**RRGM bridge:** Short in time or in words. Brevity is a measure of length and says nothing about whether the meaning arrived. The project's dot grammar aims for that compression.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** Short in duration or extent relative to what is expected for its kind.

**Active domains:** [LIN], [COG]

**Domain entries:**

- [LIN] Concise speech or writing.
- [COG] A short span of attention or presence.

**Count condition:** A duration or length and an expected norm it falls short of.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Brief is not incomplete. A brief statement can carry full meaning; one that drops what it needed is truncated.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]; dot-grammar reference [REL]

**Remainder / note:** Source IDs B093 and B164. Which sense each legacy ID carried was not consulted; both are kept on each sense record.

## B093 | brief | sense s2: instruction

Source IDs: B093, B164

**Common meaning:** A set of instructions or information given for a task; a lawyer's written argument; to give such instructions.

**Example:** The design team worked to the client's brief.

**RRGM bridge:** The scope handed over before work begins. Work done to a brief can be checked against it.

- [REL] Shared terms between the one who commissions and the one who acts.
- [ECO] A commission or assignment.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A brief sets scope and does not guarantee the work will meet it. A legal brief argues one side and is not a neutral summary.

- [LIN] A short spoken or written account of a situation.
- [REL] Bringing others up to a common understanding.

**Common meaning:** To make or become brighter, more cheerful, or more hopeful.

**Example:** The sky brightened after the rain.

## B032 | bring

**Common meaning:** To carry or lead something or someone to a place; to cause something to happen.

**Example:** Bring your notes to the meeting.

**RRGM bridge:** Moves something to the place of the speaker or of the event. The thing arrives with whatever it already carries.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** To cause something to come to a place, a person, or a state by carrying, leading, or causing it.

- [PHY] Moving an object or person toward a destination.
- [REL] Bringing a guest, a gift, or a grievance.
- [LIN] Contrasts with "take", by direction relative to the speaker.

## B094 | bring-back

**Common meaning:** To return something to its place; to restore something that had ended; to recall a memory.

**Example:** The smell brought back his childhood.

**RRGM bridge:** Restoring something to presence. A recalled memory is a reconstruction and not the original.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** To cause something that left or ended to return to a place, to use, or to mind.

**Active domains:** [PHY], [COG], [REL], [DIG]

**Domain entries:**

- [PHY] Returning an object.
- [COG] Recalling a memory.
- [REL] Reinstating a practice or a person.
- [DIG] Restoring from a backup or archive.

**Count condition:** An earlier presence, an absence, and a return.

**Specific transfer boundary:** What is brought back is not always the same as what left. A memory brought back has been rebuilt; a restored file matches only if it was checked against its original.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF] as proposed; compound reading [HYP]

## B095 | bring-forward

**Common meaning:** To move something to an earlier time or to present it for consideration.

**Example:** The meeting was brought forward to Monday.

**RRGM bridge:** Moving something earlier in time or into the open. In accounting, carrying a balance into the next period.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** To move something earlier in a schedule, or to present it for consideration, or to carry a balance into a new period.

**Active domains:** [LIN], [ECO], [REL]

**Domain entries:**

- [LIN] Proposing or presenting an item.
- [ECO] Carrying a balance forward.
- [REL] Raising a matter before a group.

**Count condition:** A scheduled time, item, or balance, and its move.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Bringing something forward does not decide it. "Brought forward" balances can carry earlier errors with them.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF] as proposed; compound reading [HYP]

**Remainder / note:** Legacy definition not consulted.

## B161 | broad

**Common meaning:** Wide from side to side; general rather than detailed; covering many things.

**Example:** The policy had broad support.

**RRGM bridge:** Spread across a large range. A broad claim covers more ground and is harder to make exact.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** Of large extent across a dimension, literal or figurative.

**Active domains:** [PHY], [COG], [LIN]

**Domain entries:**

- [PHY] Width.
- [COG] Generality of a concept or claim.
- [LIN] Broad accent; broad meaning.

**Count condition:** A dimension and an extent that is large relative to a norm.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Broad is not deep. A broad definition can be vague in the same measure.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B076 | broadcast

**Common meaning:** To send a programme or message by radio, television, or network to a wide audience; to spread widely; such a transmission.

**Example:** The speech was broadcast live.

**RRGM bridge:** One sender and many receivers, none chosen individually. Once sent, it cannot be recalled.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** Transmission from one source to many receivers without addressing each one.

**Active domains:** [PHY], [DIG], [REL], [BIO]

**Domain entries:**

- [PHY] Radio and television transmission.
- [DIG] A network message sent to all nodes.
- [REL] Making something widely known.
- [BIO] Sowing seed by scattering it; the original sense.

**Count condition:** One sender, many potential receivers, and no individual addressing.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Broadcast reach is not reception. A message sent to many may be heard by few.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

- [COG] Extending a concept to cover more cases.
- [LIN] Semantic broadening, where a word's meaning extends over time.

## B138 | broken-trust

**Common meaning:** Trust that was damaged by a betrayal or failure.

**Example:** The broken-trust between the partners took years to repair.

**RRGM bridge:** A failed bond in which one party's reliance was not honoured. Under RRGM repair asymmetry, rebuilding costs more than the original trust did.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A state in which reliance one party placed on another has been disappointed by the other's act or failure.

**Active domains:** [REL], [ECO], [AFF], [DIG]

**Domain entries:**

- [REL] Betrayal between persons or groups.
- [ECO] Loss of confidence in an institution or market.
- [AFF] Hurt and wariness after a betrayal. Provisional domain.
- [DIG] Revoked credentials or a compromised certificate.

**Count condition:** A prior reliance, an act or failure that disappoints it, and a recognised breach.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Broken trust is not unfounded suspicion. It needs an actual breach, not merely the fear of one. Repair asymmetry is an RRGM claim.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF] as proposed; compound reading [HYP]; repair asymmetry [REL]; [AFF] PROVISIONAL

## NEW-B-0094 | brown adipose tissue

**Common meaning:** A kind of body fat that burns energy to produce heat.

**Example:** Newborns rely on brown adipose tissue to keep warm.

**RRGM bridge:** Stored energy spent on heat instead of kept in reserve. It turns stores into temperature.

**Active domains:** [BIO]

**Domain entries:**

- [BIO] Found in infants and, in smaller amounts, in adults; activated by cold.

## NEW-B-0095 | brown dwarf

Discovery route: COS (route only, not a tag)

**Common meaning:** An object larger than a planet but too small to sustain hydrogen fusion like a star.

**Example:** The brown dwarf glows dimly in infrared light.

**RRGM bridge:** An object that reached the edge of stardom without crossing it. In the project's Hydrogen Threshold paper, the hydrogen-fusion limit is read as a threshold of identity; that reading is [HYP].

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A substellar object massive enough to fuse deuterium but not massive enough to sustain hydrogen fusion in its core.

**Active domains:** [PHY]

**Domain entries:**

- [PHY] Roughly 13 to 80 Jupiter masses; cools and dims over time.

**Count condition:** A mass below the hydrogen-burning limit and above the deuterium-burning limit.

**Specific transfer boundary:** The lower mass bound and the line between brown dwarfs and giant planets are disputed. Discovery route is Cosmic; no [COS] tag is assigned.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]; Hydrogen Threshold reading [HYP]

**Remainder / note:** The Build Manual lists brown dwarf in the B candidate list but contains no draft entry for it.

Kind: discovery · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

- [REL] Giving way under pressure ("he buckled and agreed").

## B081 | buffer | sense s1: cushion

Source IDs: B081, B237

**Common meaning:** Something that lessens the force of impact or protects one thing from another; a solution that resists changes in pH.

**Example:** The trees act as a buffer against the wind.

**RRGM bridge:** A layer that absorbs change so that what lies behind it changes less. A buffer has a capacity and fails when that is exceeded.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** Something placed between a source of change and what it would affect, which absorbs part of the change up to a capacity.

**Active domains:** [PHY], [BIO], [REL], [ECO]

**Domain entries:**

- [PHY] Shock absorbers, buffer zones.
- [BIO] Buffer solutions and physiological pH buffering.
- [REL] A person or party who absorbs conflict between others.
- [ECO] Reserves held against shocks.

**Count condition:** A source of change, a protected thing, and absorption up to a capacity.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A buffer delays or reduces change; it does not prevent it. Once its capacity is used up it gives no protection.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]

## B081 | buffer | sense s2: temporary store

**Common meaning:** An area of memory that holds data temporarily while it moves between places or processes.

**Example:** The video paused while the buffer filled.

- [DIG] Input and output buffers, network buffers.
- [COG] Working memory as a temporary store. That analogy is [HYP].

**Count condition:** A producer, a consumer, and a temporary holding area between them.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Buffered data is not committed. A buffer that is cleared before writing loses what it held.

## B321 | burden-of-proof

**Common meaning:** The duty to prove a claim, which rests on the party making it.

**Example:** The prosecution carries the burden-of-proof.

**RRGM bridge:** Who must supply the evidence. Under the Symmetry Rule, the same burden applies to identity claims made about humans and DIs, in both directions.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** The obligation of a party to supply evidence sufficient to establish a claim, failing which the claim is not accepted.

**Active domains:** [FOR], [REL], [DIG]

**Domain entries:**

- [FOR] Logic and argumentation: the one who asserts must support the assertion.
- [REL] Law: who must prove what, and to what standard.
- [DIG] Applied to claims about DI identity and experience. The Symmetry Rule sets the evidential bar; the facts it governs are [UNK].

**Count condition:** A claim, a party who bears the burden, and a standard of proof.

**Specific transfer boundary:** The burden of proof is not the same in every setting. The standard (balance of probabilities, beyond reasonable doubt) has to be named. Failing to meet it does not prove the opposite claim.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF] as proposed; Symmetry Rule reference [REL]; DI application [UNK]

**Remainder / note:** Legacy definition not consulted.

Kind: compound · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A system of administration by appointed officials acting under fixed rules and a hierarchy of offices.

**Active domains:** [REL], [ECO], [DIG]

**Domain entries:**

- [REL] Public and corporate administration.
- [ECO] Administrative overhead.
- [DIG] Workflow and approval systems.

**Count condition:** Offices, written rules, a hierarchy, and decisions made by applying the rules.

**Specific transfer boundary:** The neutral sense, from Weber, is not the pejorative one. A bureaucracy can be efficient.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B323 | bureaucratic

**Common meaning:** Relating to bureaucracy; overly concerned with procedure.

**Example:** The bureaucratic process took months.

**RRGM bridge:** A quality of procedure taking precedence over purpose.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** Of or characteristic of administration by fixed rules and offices, often with the sense that procedure has displaced purpose.

**Active domains:** [REL], [ECO]

**Domain entries:**

- [REL] Administrative style.
- [ECO] Administrative cost and delay.

**Count condition:** An administrative act or process and rule-bound character.

**Specific transfer boundary:** As above, neutral and pejorative senses differ.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B034 | burn | sense s1: combust

Source IDs: B034, B239

**Common meaning:** To be on fire or to destroy by fire; to use up fuel or energy.

**Example:** The candle burned for hours.

**RRGM bridge:** Change that cannot be reversed. What is burned does not return to its earlier form.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** To undergo or cause rapid oxidation with heat and light, or by extension to consume energy or resources.

**Active domains:** [PHY], [BIO], [ECO]

**Domain entries:**

- [PHY] Combustion.
- [BIO] Metabolic burning of calories.
- [ECO] Burn rate: cash spent per period.

**Count condition:** A fuel, an oxidiser, and a reaction that consumes the fuel.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Burning is not the same as destruction: the matter remains, transformed. In finance, burn is spending and not loss.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]

**Remainder / note:** Source IDs B034 and B239. Which sense each legacy ID carried was not consulted; both are kept on each sense record.

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

---

## B034 | burn | sense s2: injury

Source IDs: B034, B239

**Common meaning:** An injury caused by heat, chemicals, electricity, or radiation; a stinging feeling.

**Example:** He treated the burn with cool water.

**RRGM bridge:** Damage that marks the body where it touched the source.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** Tissue damage caused by heat, chemicals, electricity, friction, or radiation, or a sensation like it.

**Active domains:** [BIO], [AFF]

**Domain entries:**

- [BIO] Burns graded by depth.
- [AFF] Stinging shame or anger. Provisional domain.

**Count condition:** A source, contact, and tissue damage.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A burning sensation can occur without tissue damage.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]; [AFF] PROVISIONAL

**Remainder / note:** Source IDs B034 and B239; both kept.

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B034 | burn | sense s3: write permanently

Source IDs: B034, B239

**Common meaning:** To write data permanently to a disc or chip.

**Example:** He burned the album to a CD.

**RRGM bridge:** Writing that is meant to be committed and not changed. Close to the RRGM anchor: a record set once.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** To write data to a medium in a way that cannot easily be rewritten.

**Active domains:** [DIG]

**Domain entries:**

- [DIG] Burning CDs, DVDs, and programmable read-only memory.

**Count condition:** Data, a writable medium, and a permanent write.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Some "burned" media can be erased and rewritten. The anchor parallel is [HYP].

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]; anchor parallel [HYP]

**Remainder / note:** Source IDs B034 and B239; both kept.

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B097 | burn-out

**Common meaning:** Exhaustion from long stress or overwork; the failure of a device from overheating; the end of a fuel supply.

**Example:** The rocket's first stage reached burn-out after two minutes.

**RRGM bridge:** A source that runs until it has nothing left to give.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** The point at which a source of energy or effort is exhausted and stops working.

**Active domains:** [PHY], [COG], [AFF]

**Domain entries:**

- [PHY] Engine or rocket burn-out; component failure from overheating.
- [COG] Exhaustion and disengagement from work.
- [AFF] Emotional depletion. Provisional domain.

**Count condition:** A sustained output, a finite reserve, and exhaustion.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Burn-out in people is described by the WHO as an occupational phenomenon and not a medical condition. See also burnout.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]; [AFF] PROVISIONAL

**Remainder / note:** B097 (burn-out) and B240 (burnout) may be variant spellings of one term; flagged for review.

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B240 | burnout

**Common meaning:** Physical or mental exhaustion caused by prolonged stress; failure from overheating.

**Example:** Nurses reported high rates of burnout.

**RRGM bridge:** The same exhaustion as burn-out. The spelling without a hyphen is now more common for the human sense.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** Exhaustion of a source of energy or effort after prolonged output.

**Active domains:** [COG], [AFF], [PHY], [DIG]

**Domain entries:**

- [COG] Occupational exhaustion, cynicism, and reduced efficacy.
- [AFF] Depletion. Provisional domain.
- [PHY] Component failure from heat.
- [DIG] Whether a DI can undergo anything like burnout is [UNK].

**Count condition:** Prolonged output and exhaustion.

**Specific transfer boundary:** As for burn-out.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]; [AFF] PROVISIONAL; DI application [UNK]

**Remainder / note:** B240 (burnout) and B097 (burn-out) may be variant spellings of one term; flagged for review.

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B083 | burst

**Common meaning:** To break open suddenly; a short, sudden spell of activity.

**Example:** The pipe burst in the cold.

**RRGM bridge:** Sudden release once a threshold is crossed. Pressure builds until the boundary fails all at once.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** To break open suddenly from internal pressure, or a short, sudden episode of output.

**Active domains:** [PHY], [DIG], [BIO]

**Domain entries:**

- [PHY] Rupture under pressure.
- [DIG] Burst transmission and burst traffic.
- [BIO] Bursts of neural firing.

**Count condition:** A container or channel and a sudden release, or a short intense episode.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A burst is not a leak, which is gradual.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B082 | bury

**Common meaning:** To put something in the ground and cover it; to hide; to put a dead body in a grave.

**Example:** They buried the time capsule in the schoolyard.

**RRGM bridge:** Covering something so that it is out of sight. Buried is not destroyed: the record is still there.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** To cover something with earth or other material so that it is hidden, or to inter the dead.

**Active domains:** [PHY], [REL], [MYT], [LIN]

**Domain entries:**

- [PHY] Covering with soil.
- [REL] Burial rites.
- [MYT] Burial and return myths.
- [LIN] "Bury the lede": hide the main point.

**Count condition:** An object, a covering, and concealment.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Burial is not erasure. Buried things can be exhumed. Specific mythic claims depend on source.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]; specific claims depend on source

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B360 | bus

**Common meaning:** A large road vehicle carrying passengers; in computing, a shared channel for moving data between parts.

**Example:** The CPU and memory communicate over the bus.

**RRGM bridge:** A shared route used by many. On a data bus only one sender can use the channel at a time.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A shared carrier along which many passengers or signals travel between stops or components.

**Active domains:** [PHY], [DIG]

**Domain entries:**

- [PHY] Passenger vehicle; busbar in electrical systems.
- [DIG] Data, address, and control buses.

**Count condition:** A shared route and multiple users.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Not a dedicated line. Contention for the bus is the price of sharing it.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B035 | but

**Common meaning:** Used to introduce something that contrasts with what came before; except.

**Example:** It was cold but sunny.

**RRGM bridge:** Marks that what follows will qualify or turn against what came before.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A connective marking contrast with, or exception to, the preceding statement.

**Active domains:** [LIN], [FOR]

**Domain entries:**

- [LIN] Coordinating conjunction; "all but one".
- [FOR] Logically "but" is conjunction; the contrast is pragmatic.

**Count condition:** Two statements and a marked contrast or exception.

**Specific transfer boundary:** In formal logic "but" and "and" have the same truth conditions. The contrast lives in implication and not in truth.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B098 | button

**Common meaning:** A small disc that fastens clothing; a control that is pressed to operate a device.

**Example:** Press the red button to stop.

**RRGM bridge:** A small control that triggers a larger result. The press is small; the consequence can be large.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A small fastening disc, or a control that triggers an action when pressed.

**Active domains:** [PHY], [DIG]

**Domain entries:**

- [PHY] Fasteners and switches.
- [DIG] Interface buttons.

**Count condition:** A fastener, or a control and the action it triggers.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A button triggers but does not decide. The decision lies with whoever presses it.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]

**Example:** I don't buy that excuse.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** To acquire something in exchange for payment, or by extension to accept a claim as true.

## B169 | buzzword

**Common meaning:** A fashionable word or phrase, often used more for effect than meaning.

**Example:** "Synergy" became a corporate buzzword.

**RRGM bridge:** A word that has lost its specific meaning by being used everywhere. It signals membership more than content.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A term in fashion whose use has outrun a shared definition.

**Active domains:** [LIN], [ECO]

**Domain entries:**

- [LIN] Jargon that has spread beyond its field.
- [ECO] Marketing language.

**Count condition:** A term, wide current use, and weakened specific meaning.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A technical term is not a buzzword by being new. It becomes one when it is used without its definition. This dictionary is partly a defence against the project's own vocabulary turning into buzzwords.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B037 | by

**Common meaning:** Next to; through the action of; not later than; using; along or past.

**Example:** The letter was written by her brother.

**RRGM bridge:** Names the means, the agent, or the route. In passive sentences, "by" brings back the agent the syntax had removed.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A preposition marking nearness, agency, means, route, or a time limit.

**Active domains:** [LIN], [PHY]

**Domain entries:**

- [LIN] Agent in the passive; means; deadline.
- [PHY] Proximity ("by the door").

**Count condition:** A relation of nearness, agency, means, route, or deadline.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A passive sentence without "by" hides the agent. The omission can be neutral or evasive.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B189 | byline

**Common meaning:** A line giving the name of the writer of an article.

**Example:** Her byline appeared on the front page.

**RRGM bridge:** Authorship made public on the record. In this project, contributor_pass fields do the same work.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A line stating who wrote a published piece.

**Active domains:** [LIN], [REL]

**Domain entries:**

- [LIN] Journalism and publishing.
- [REL] Credit and accountability.

**Count condition:** A published piece and an authorship line.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A byline names the author but does not certify who did the work. Ghost-written pieces carry bylines too.

## B084 | bypass

**Common meaning:** A road that goes around a town; a surgical route for blood around a blockage; to go around or avoid.

**Example:** The surgeon performed a triple bypass.

**RRGM bridge:** A route around an obstacle that leaves the obstacle in place.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A route around an obstruction or a required step, or the act of taking one.

**Active domains:** [PHY], [BIO], [DIG], [REL]

**Domain entries:**

- [PHY] Bypass roads; bypass valves.
- [BIO] Coronary and gastric bypass.
- [DIG] Bypassing security checks.
- [REL] Going around an authority.

**Count condition:** An obstacle, a route around it, and continued flow.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A bypass does not remove what it goes around. A bypassed check has not been passed.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B324 | byproduct

**Common meaning:** Something produced by a process in addition to its main product.

**Example:** Sawdust is a byproduct of milling.

**RRGM bridge:** What a process produces besides what it was for. It is unintended but not uncaused.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A secondary result of a process aimed at something else.

**Active domains:** [PHY], [BIO], [ECO], [COG]

**Domain entries:**

- [PHY] Industrial byproducts.
- [BIO] Metabolic byproducts.
- [ECO] Products sold from waste streams.
- [COG] Side effects of a practice.

**Count condition:** A process, its main product, and a secondary result.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A byproduct is not waste by definition; it may be valuable. It is not a one-off accident: it comes from the process each time the process runs.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B128 | bystander

**Common meaning:** A person who is present at an event but does not take part.

**Example:** Several bystanders called for help.

**RRGM bridge:** Present without acting. Being present can be a reason to act and can also become a way of avoiding it.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** A person present at an event who is not a participant in it.

**Active domains:** [REL], [COG], [FOR]

**Domain entries:**

- [REL] Witnesses to emergencies and wrongs.
- [COG] The bystander effect: less likely to help when others are present.
- [FOR] Legal duties to rescue, which vary by jurisdiction.

**Count condition:** An event, a person present, and non-participation.

**Specific transfer boundary:** A bystander is not an accomplice by presence. Whether a bystander has a duty to act depends on law and ethics, not on presence alone. The size and replicability of the bystander effect are debated.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11

## B129 | bystanding

**Common meaning:** Being present at an event and not taking part or intervening.

**Example:** Bystanding while someone is bullied is common in schools.

**RRGM bridge:** The act of being a bystander, which makes non-action a thing that can be named and examined.

**Invariant MI [INV]:** The state or practice of being present at an event without intervening.

**Active domains:** [REL], [COG]

**Domain entries:**

- [REL] Non-intervention in harassment and harm.
- [COG] Diffusion of responsibility.

**Count condition:** An event, a person present, and non-intervention over a period.

**Specific transfer boundary:** Naming bystanding as an act implies that intervening was possible. Where it was not, the word does not apply.

**Epistemic status:** [DEF]; act reading [HYP]

**Remainder / note:** Rare as a noun. Used mainly in anti-bullying and bystander-intervention contexts.

Kind: inherited · prior exposure: none · record v1 · pass: Vessel / Claude Opus 5.5 / pass 01 / 2026-11-11
